Casimir Radial Parts via Matsuki Decomposition
Philip Schlösser, Mikhail Isachenkov

Abstract
. We use Matsuki’s decomposition for symmetric pairs ( G , H ) (G,H) of (not necessarily compact) reductive Lie groups to construct the radial parts for invariant differential operators acting on matrix-spherical functions. As an application, we employ this machinery to formulate an alternative, mathematically rigorous approach to obtaining radial parts of Casimir operators that appear in the theory of conformal blocks, which avoids poorly defined analytical continuations from the compact quotient cases. To exemplify how this works, after reviewing the presentation of conformal 4-point correlation functions via matrix-spherical functions for the corresponding symmetric pair, we for the first time provide a complete analysis of the Casimir radial part decomposition in the case of Lorentzian signature. As another example, we revisit the Casimir reduction in the case of conformal blocks for two scalar defects of equal dimension. We argue that Matsuki’s decomposition thus provides a proper mathematical framework for analysing the correspondence between Casimir equations and the Calogero–Sutherland-type models, first discovered by one of the authors and Schomerus.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
33C55, 33C67, 33C80, 43A90, 81T40

1.
Introduction

A matrix-spherical function for two (topological, real Lie, complex Lie, algebraic) groups
(G,H)
with
H\leq G
finite-dimensional
H
-representations
V,W
is a (continuous, smooth, holomorphic, regular) function

f:\,G\to\Hom(V,W)
satisfying

\forall g\in G,h,h^{\prime}\in H:\quad f(hgh^{\prime})=\pi_{W}(h)f(g)\pi_{V}(h^{\prime}).

The theory of matrix-spherical functions, and the matrix-valued
orthogonal polynomials they give rise to, has been well studied, with references including
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
. Considerable attention has
been given to matrix-spherical functions for symmetric pairs
(G,K)
,
i.e. when there is an involutive isomorphism
\theta
of
G
and
K=G^{\theta}
is compact.

However, the literature becomes more sparse when
we allow
K
to be non-compact. The reason for this is that the well-studied cases
correspond to the situation (and slight variations thereof) that the
reductive group
(G,K,\theta,B)
(see e.g.
[
4
, Section VII.2]
) is quotiented by its maximal compact subgroup
K
, and thus there is
an Abelian subgroup
A
such that

G=KAK

[
4
, Section VII.3]
. This allows to uniquely reconstruct
(G,K)
-matrix-spherical functions
from their restrictions to
A
and can then be leveraged to decompose invariant differential operators into matrix-valued differential operators on
A
,
their
radial parts
, as
is done in
[
2
]
, following the earlier works of Harish-Chandra.

The proof that
G
decomposes as
KAK
quite crucially relies on the
fact that
K
is compact, and such a statement at face value is usually wrong otherwise. Actually, as is shown in
[
5
]
, the best we can hope
for with a general symmetric pair
(G,H)
is

G=\overline{\bigcup_{i}HC_{i}H}

for finitely many “affine tori”
C_{i}
, i.e. cosets with respect
to an Abelian subgroup. Fortunately, it turns out that in this case matrix-spherical functions
are still determined by their restrictions to all of the
C_{i}
. In the present
work we will take this story one step further and explore how invariant differential operators, in particular the quadratic Casimir element, decompose in such setting.

This has a direct application to conformal field theory (CFT), more
specifically the theory of conformal blocks. As is explained in

[
6
, Section 3]
, conformal blocks for (Euclidean) 4-point correlators
can be described as matrix-spherical functions for

(SO(d+1,1)_{0},SO(1,1)_{0}\times SO(d))
.
1
1

1

Notice, however, that a rigorous treatment of correlation functions in a CFT also needs to take into account their distributional nature. In the present paper we choose not to work in this generality. See e.g.
[
7
,
8
]
for the recent relevant work in this direction and a review of earlier physics literature.
In the scalar case (i.e.

SO(d)
acting trivially, and
SO(1,1)_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}_{>0}
acting by characters), the action of the quadratic Casimir operator was first determined in
[
9
]
. The authors of
[
10
]
then showed that a change of coordinates transforms the resulting differential operator into the Hamiltonian of the Calogero–Sutherland model for
BC_{2}
, with parameters
that refer not only to multiplicities of a relevant root system, but also to the characters by which
SO(1,1)_{0}
acts from the left and from the right.

The Calogero–Sutherland model is a super-integrable model with quite some history. In particular, it has been extensively studied by
Heckman and Opdam (see e.g.
[
11
]
and references therein) and has been
known to capture the Casimir action on zonal spherical functions
for the more conventionally studied symmetric pairs
(G,K)
, where
K
has to be compact. This connection, however, is not enough
to explain the result from
[
10
]
, as

SO(1,1)_{0}\times SO(d)
is non-compact, and as the conformal blocks
in question aren’t zonal spherical functions. What we will show in present paper is that,
with Matsuki’s decomposition in hand, the steps from
[
11
]
can be reproduced very closely to yield parallel
results in the vastly generalised setting, including for example the case of Lorentzian kinematics, where the configuration space of points on the group has richer structure compared to the Euclidean situation.
As a matter of fact, we will develop most of the theory for the general symmetric pair context, then specialize to a general indefinite orthogonal group
G=SO(p+1,q+1)
, and only further down the road specialize to Euclidean and Lorentzian signatures (
q=0,1
), once the real rank of
G
will start to affect the amount of needed calculations in an essential way.

Lastly, conformal blocks for two defects of the same dimension can
also be described as spherical functions, namely for
(SO(d+1,1)_{0},SO(d-p)\times SO(p+1,1)_{0})
. We will see that this setup also falls within the domain of validity for
Matsuki’s decomposition, so that we will be able to similarly use it in the
radial part decomposition of the quadratic Casimir element, clarifying
the appearance of the Calogero–Sutherland model
in this context, which was earlier argued in

[
12
]
.

Let us stress that the issue of having a properly defined Cartan decomposition in non-compact case is also important from the physics perspective, perhaps contrary to what might seem at the first glance.
Being sometimes perceived more as a mathematical nuisance, it is usually glossed over in the physics literature by declaring it to be a more or less straightforward ’analytical continuation’ from the compact case. However, as is frequently the case with going from compact spaces to non-compact ones, the ’boundary questions’ actually cause trouble and turn out to be rather non-trivial mathematically, especially in treating lower-dimensional group orbits. Notice that, despite the possible lower dimensionality of such strata, good analytical control over them is of relevance, since various such regions might carry information about singularities of the physical correlation functions.

As we have just reviewed above, bits and pieces of this radial part analysis, mostly but not exclusively in Euclidean setting, have appeared in physics literature before, see, in particular,
[
13
,
6
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
. Spherical functions relevant for Lorentzian analysis
in the scalar case were also recently discussed in
[
17
]
. However, the mathematically rigorous description of the Casimir reduction in the setting pertinent to the four-point (spinning) conformal blocks, including the case of Lorentzian signature, was, as far as we are aware, up to now missing from the literature. In particular, a careful analysis of the subtleties of the
KAK
-type matrix decomposition in non-compact case that we provide in this paper using Matsuki’s theory is, to our knowledge, new.
2
2

2

For example, in
[
16
]
the existence of such a decomposition is attributed to the Gel’fand pair property of
(G,H)
. However, we are not aware of any general statements yielding such a group factorisation for a given non-compact Gel’fand pair.

This, besides the wish to streamline and clean up mathematical details, might be seen as the main motivation behind this work on the physics side. It puts the
KAK
-type decompositions used in the Calogero–Sutherland approach to conformal blocks on a firm mathematical ground.

The plan of the paper is as follows. In Section
2
we review some generalities on matrix-spherical functions, especially
with respect to group actions on them. We then go on, in
Section
3
to review Matsuki’s (
[
5
]
) theory of decompositions
in the special case where the two involutions
\sigma=\tau
and
the two subgroups
H=L
are equal. In Section
4

we develop this into a theory of radial part decompositions and compute the radial part of the quadratic Casimir element.
In Section
5
we then pivot to applications in the theory of
conformal blocks and explain how conformal blocks for 4-point correlation
functions can be viewed as matrix-spherical functions in the sense
defined earlier. In particular, in Section
6
we do a thorough
analysis of Matsuki’s theory for the group
SO(p+1,q+1)_{0}
, derive
the expression of the quadratic Casimir element and match it with the
scalar (Heckman–Opdam) expression from
[
10
]

and
the spinor expression from
[
15
]
. In Section
7
we
then provide a brief discussion of defect blocks and
matching our results with
[
12
]
. We close this paper with a short summary and discussion of possible future directions.

A word about notation: most of the notation is chosen in such a way
that it is compatible with
[
5
]
. The notation of
Section
5
that pertains to
G,\mathfrak{g}
and
various subgroups and subalgebras is more in line with the
standard treatment of the structure theory of simple Lie groups
(as presented in
[
4
]
) and
will, due to collisions with
[
5
]
’s notation, not be used
in the subsequent sections. In Subsection
6.6
(and
examples beforehand), we
introduce the scalar parameters
\alpha,\beta
, so then roots
will not be called
\alpha,\beta
, but
\gamma
.

2.
Matrix-Spherical Functions (MSFs)

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(G,H)
be a pair of groups, i.e.
G
is a group and
H\leq G
is a subgroup.
Let
(V,\pi_{V}),(W,\pi_{W})
be representations of
H
. A function
f:\,G\to\Hom(V,W)

is said to be a
matrix-spherical function
(MSF) if

\forall g\in G,h,h^{\prime}\in H:\quad f(hgh^{\prime})=\pi_{W}(h)f(g)\pi_{V}(h^{\prime}).

In the case where
G,H
are real Lie groups (complex Lie groups, algebraic groups),
we usually also require
f
to be smooth (holomorphic, regular). Write

E^{V,W}(G,H)
for the set of matrix-spherical functions for
(G,H)
with the
representations
V,W
.

In the more general case, where
W
is an
H
-bimodule, we
can make the same definition: a function
f:\,G\to W
is a MSF
if

\forall g\in G,h,h^{\prime}\in H:\quad f(hgh^{\prime})=h\cdot f(g)\cdot h^{\prime}.

However, as we cannot interpret this function as being
matrix-valued anymore, the name MSF is a misnomer. Write

E^{W}(G,H)
for the functions thus described.

We’re now going to consider the case where
(G,H)
are Lie groups and

W
an
H
-bimodule with two smooth actions.

Lemma 2.2
.

E^{W}(G,H)
has actions by
U(\mathfrak{g})^{H}
in terms of left-invariant
and right-invariant differential operators. Furthermore, these two actions are
pointwise related: for
f\in E^{W}(G,H)
(or
C^{\infty}(G,W)
more generally),
g\in G,p\in U(\mathfrak{g})^{H}
with

\Ad(g)(p)\in U(\mathfrak{g})^{H}
we have

(p\cdot f)(g)=(f\cdot\Ad(g)(p))(g).

Proof.

We first show that
C^{\infty}(G,W)
is a
(U(\mathfrak{g}),H)
-bimodule (which is derived from the left and right regular representations).

For a function
f\in C^{\infty}(G,W)
, and elements
g\in G,h,h^{\prime}\in H,X\in\mathfrak{g}

we define

\displaystyle(h\cdot f\cdot h^{\prime})(g)

\displaystyle:=(h^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot f(h^{\prime}gh)\cdot h^{-1}

\displaystyle(X\cdot f)(g)

\displaystyle:=\derivative{t}\evaluated{f(g\exp(tX))}_{t=0}

\displaystyle(f\cdot X)(g)

\displaystyle:=\derivative{t}\evaluated{f(\exp(tX)g)}_{t=0}.

Since
G
is associative, the left and right actions commute.
Furthermore, for
h\in H,p\in U(\mathfrak{g})
we have

\displaystyle h\cdot p\cdot h^{-1}\cdot f

\displaystyle=\Ad(h)(p)\cdot f

\displaystyle f\cdot h\cdot p\cdot h^{-1}

\displaystyle=f\cdot\Ad(h)(p),

so that the left actions (resp. the right actions) are compatible
with each other, which establishes that
(U(\mathfrak{g}),H)
-bimodule structure. Therefore, the invariants under the left action of
H

still have a left action of
U(\mathfrak{g})^{H}
, analogously for the
right actions. In particular, the elements that are left and right
invariant under
H
(i.e. elements of
E^{W}(G,H)
) have a left and a right
action of
U(\mathfrak{g})^{H}
. As can be seen from the definition, the left
action of
U(\mathfrak{g})
involves the right regular representation on
C^{\infty}(G)
and therefore
commutes with the left regular representation, so that it acts by left-invariant
differential operators. Similarly, the right action of
U(\mathfrak{g})

is an action by means of right-invariant differential operators.

Lastly, we show the pointwise relation between the left and right actions
without the
H
-invariance assumption, i.e. for
C^{\infty}(G,W)

and
U(\mathfrak{g})
. Let
f\in C^{\infty}(G,W)
and
X\in\mathfrak{g}
then

(X\cdot f)(g)=\derivative{t}\evaluated{f(g\exp(tX))}_{t=0}=\derivative{t}\evaluated{f(\exp(t\Ad(g)(X))g)}_{t=0}=(f\cdot\Ad(g)(X))(g).

Now assume, the claim already holds for
p\in U(\mathfrak{g})
, then we have

\displaystyle(Xp\cdot f)(g)

\displaystyle=(X\cdot(p\cdot f))(g)=((p\cdot f)\cdot\Ad(g)(X))(g)

\displaystyle=(p\cdot(f\cdot\Ad(g)(X)))(g)=((f\cdot\Ad(g)(X))\cdot\Ad(g)(p))(g)

\displaystyle=(f\cdot\Ad(g)(Xp))(g).\qed

Corollary 2.3
.

The actions from Lemma
2.2
can be restricted to

U(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
. If
G
is connected, this yields two actions of
Z(U(\mathfrak{g}))
that coincide.

Proof.

If
G
is connected,
G
-invariance is the same as
\mathfrak{g}
-invariance, so

U(\mathfrak{g})^{G}=Z(U(\mathfrak{g}))
. Let
z\in Z(U(\mathfrak{g})),g\in G

and
f\in E^{W}(G,H)
. By Lemma
2.2
, we have

(z\cdot f)(g)=(f\cdot\Ad(g)(z))(g)=(f\cdot z)(g).\qed

Corollary 2.4
.

For
p\in U(\mathfrak{h})
and
q\in U(\mathfrak{g})
and
f\in E^{W}(G,H)
we have

(pq\cdot f)(g)=(p\cdot f)(g)\cdot q,\qquad(f\cdot qp)(g)=q\cdot(f\cdot p)(g).

Proof.

We show the claim for
q
being a monomial and then use linearity. Let
X_{1}\cdots X_{r}\in\mathfrak{h}
and
p=X_{1}\cdots X_{r}
. Let
g\in G
, then

\displaystyle(q\cdot f)(g)

\displaystyle=\derivative{t_1}\cdots\derivative{t_r}\evaluated{f(g\exp(tX_1)\cdots\exp(tX_r))}_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\derivative{t_1}\cdots\derivative{t_r}\evaluated{f(g)\cdot\exp(t_1X_1)\cdots\exp(t_rX_r)}_{t_1=\cdots=t_r=0}

\displaystyle=f(g)\cdot q,

whence
(pq\cdot f)(g)=(p\cdot(q\cdot f))(g)=(p\cdot f)(g)\cdot q
as claimed. The result for the right action can
be obtained analogously.
∎

Suppose now that we can decompose a dense subset
G^{\prime}
of
G
as follows:

(2.1)

G^{\prime}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}HC_{i}H

where
C_{i}\subseteq G
are weakly embedded submanifolds (in our story these will turn out to be tori or right cosets of tori), i.e. the
identity map
C_{i}\hookrightarrow G
is an immersion. Parallel to the
usual treatment, a spherical function is then determined by its
restrictions to these tori
C_{i}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

The map
|_{C}:\,\,E^{W}(G,H)\to\prod_{i\in I}C^{\infty}(C_{i},W)

given by

f\mapsto(f|_{C_{i}})_{i\in I}

is injective.

Proof.

Let
f,f^{\prime}
with
f|_{C}=f^{\prime}|_{C}
, i.e.
f|_{C_{i}}=f^{\prime}|_{C_{i}}
for all
i\in I
. Since
f,f^{\prime}
are matrix-spherical, they agree on the corresponding
H\times H
-orbits
as well, i.e. on
HC_{i}H
for all
i\in I
, so in particular on

\bigcup_{i\in I}HC_{i}H=G^{\prime}.

Since both are continuous and
G^{\prime}
is dense in
G
, we have
f=f^{\prime}
.
∎

Definition 2.6
.

For
i\in I
define

\displaystyle N_{C_{i}}

\displaystyle:=\{(h,h^{\prime})\in H^{2}\mid hC_{i}h^{\prime-1}=C_{i}\}

\displaystyle Z_{C_{i}}

\displaystyle:=\{(h,h^{\prime})\in H^{2}\mid\forall x\in C_{i}:\quad hxh^{\prime-1}=x\}

and
J_{C_{i}}:=N_{C_{i}}/Z_{C_{i}}
. They are all groups.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
f\in E^{W}(G,H)
and
i\in I
, then

f|_{C_{i}}\in C^{\infty}(C_{i},W^{Z_{C_{i}}})^{J_{C_{i}}}.

Proof.

Let
g\in C_{i}
and
(h,h^{\prime})\in N_{C_{i}}
, then

f(hgh^{\prime-1})=h\cdot f(g)\cdot h^{\prime-1}=(h,h^{\prime})\cdot f(x).

For the case where
(h,h^{\prime})\in Z_{C_{i}}
we have
hgh^{\prime-1}=g
and hence

f(g)\in W^{Z_{C_{i}}}
. Thus we see that
f|_{C_{i}}
intertwines the actions of

N_{C_{i}}
, which pass to the quotient
J_{C_{i}}
.
∎

3.
Matsuki’s Double Coset Decomposition

We are now going to quote some results from
[
5
]
that will be relevant
later. Note that Matsuki considers two (generally) different involutions

\sigma,\tau
on
G
, whereas in this paper we shall always assume
\sigma=\tau
.

Let
(G,H)
be a symmetric pair of Lie groups, i.e.
H\leq G
a Lie subgroup
and
\sigma\in\Aut(G)
an involution such that
(G^{\sigma})_{0}\leq H\leq G^{\sigma}
,
where we assume that
HG_{0}H=G
. We shall also assume that
(G,K,\theta,B)

is a reductive Lie group (see e.g.
[
4
, Section VII.2]
), where
\theta
and
\sigma
(’s derivative) commute.

We shall also assume that
B
is invariant under
\sigma
, which is no
restriction by the following observation:

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
(G,K,\theta,B)
be a reductive Lie group and let
S\leq\Aut(G)
be a
finite subgroup (the derivative of) whose action commutes with
\theta
.
Then
B
can without loss of generality be chosen to be
S
-invariant.

Proof.

If we choose

B^{\prime}(X,Y):=\sum_{\sigma\in S}B(\sigma(X),\sigma(Y))

instead of
B
, we obtain a real inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
that
is invariant under
\Ad(G),\theta,S
, which is still positive(negative)-definite on
\mathfrak{p}
(
\mathfrak{k}
), and under which
\mathfrak{p}

and
\mathfrak{k}
are still orthogonal. In other words:
(G,K,\theta,B^{\prime})

is still a reductive Lie group.
∎

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{t}
be a maximal commutative subalgebra of
\mathfrak{k}^{-\sigma}
and extend
\mathfrak{t}
to a maximal commutative subalgebra

\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}
.
The subgroup
C:=\exp(\mathfrak{c})=:\exp(\mathfrak{a})T
is called a
fundamental Cartan subset
.

Definition 3.3
.

Given a fundamental Cartan subset
C
as above, a subset

C^{\prime}=\exp(\mathfrak{c}')t
of
G
is called a
standard Cartan subset

if

(i)

t\in T
and
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}\leq\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
is a commutative subalgebra;

(ii)

\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\oplus\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
where

\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\leq\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{a}\leq\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
;

(iii)

\dim(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})=\dim(\mathfrak{c})
.

Two standard Cartan subsets
\exp(\mathfrak{a}'_1)T^{\prime}_{1}
and
\exp(\mathfrak{a}'_2)T^{\prime}_{2}
are said to be
conjugate
if there are
h,h^{\prime}\in K\cap H
such that
hT^{\prime}_{1}h^{\prime-1}=T^{\prime}_{2}
. In particular,
any two Cartan subsets with the same
T^{\prime}
are conjugate.

Example 3.4
.

For the case of
\sigma=\theta
and
H=K
, i.e. the case of
non-compact symmetric spaces, we pick

\mathfrak{t}:=0
and
\mathfrak{a}
as a maximal commutative
subalgebra of
\mathfrak{p}
. Then the fundamental Cartan subset

C
is the analytic subgroup
A
of
\mathfrak{a}
, i.e. the torus
one finds in the usual treatment for this case, e.g.
[
4
, Theorem 6.46]
. There are also no other standard Cartan subsets (with respect to
C
) as
\mathfrak{a}
is already a maximal
commutative subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}
.

From now on, we shall fix a fundamental Cartan subset
C\leq G
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
C^{\prime}=\exp(\mathfrak{c}')t
be a standard Cartan subset, then

\Ad(t)
is an involution of
\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
. In particular,
C^{\prime}
and
tC^{\prime}t^{-1}
are
conjugate standard Cartan subsets.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
, so
that
X,\Ad(t^{-1})(X)\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}
. Note
that
t\in\exp(\mathfrak{k}^{-\sigma})
, whence
\sigma(t^{-1})=t
.
Consequently, we have

-\Ad(t^{-1})(X)=\sigma(\Ad(t^{-1})(X))=\Ad(t)(\sigma(X))=-\Ad(t)(X),

which implies
\Ad(t^{-2})(X)=X
. Therefore we have

\Ad(t)(X),X\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}
, so that

\Ad(t)(X)\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}).

Now, note that
tC^{\prime}t^{-1}=t\exp(\mathfrak{c}')=\exp(\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{c}'))t

and that
\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
decomposes as

\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\oplus\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime})

since
t\in\exp(\mathfrak{t})
commutes with
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\leq\mathfrak{t}
.
Therefore,
tC^{\prime}t^{-1}
is also a standard Cartan subset that shares its
“compact part”
\exp(\mathfrak{t}')t
with
C^{\prime}
and is therefore
conjugate to
C^{\prime}
.
∎

Definition 3.6
.

The set
G_{\mathrm{ss}}
of all
semisimple elements
of
G
is the set of all
g\in G
such that
\sigma\circ\Ad(g)\circ\sigma\circ\Ad(g)^{-1}
is a semisimple Lie algebra
automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
. Let

G_{\mathrm{rs}}:=\{g\in G_{\mathrm{ss}}\mid g^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(g)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})\text{ is commutative}\}

be the set of all
regular semisimple elements
. Both are dense in
G
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
C^{\prime}=\exp(\mathfrak{c}')t
be a standard Cartan subset and let

x\in C^{\prime}
. Then

\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})=\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}

if and only if
x\in G_{rs}
.

Proof.

“
\Leftarrow
:
“
\supset
”: Write
x=\exp(X)t
and let
Y\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
. Then

\Ad(x^{-1})(Y)=\Ad(t^{-1})\exp(-\ad(X))(Y)=\Ad(t^{-1})(Y)\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}

since
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}\leq\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
by definition.

“
\subseteq
”: Since
x=\exp(X)t
is regular,
\mathfrak{s}:=\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
is commutative and contains
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
by
the observation above. For
Y\in\mathfrak{s}
we thus have
\ad(X)(Y)=0
,
so that

\Ad(t^{-1})(Y)=\Ad(x^{-1})\exp(-\ad(X))(Y)=\Ad(x^{-1})(Y)\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}.

Consequently,

\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}\leq\mathfrak{s}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}),

where
\mathfrak{s}
is commutative and
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
is maximally
commutative. Consequently,
\mathfrak{s}\leq\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
.

“
\Rightarrow
”: If
x
is not regular, then
\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
is not commutative. Therefore, it cannot
be
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
.
∎

Theorem 3.8

(
[
5
, Theorem 3(i–iii)]
)
.

Let
G,H,K,\sigma,\theta
as before. Fix a fundamental Cartan subset and
let
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
be representatives of all the conjugacy classes
of standard Cartan subsets. Then

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{ss}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in I}HC_{i}H

\displaystyle G_{\mathrm{rs}}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}H(C_{i}\cap G_{\mathrm{rs}})H.

This establishes a decomposition as in (
2.1
).
Every element of the group
J_{C_{i}}
we defined for such a case can
be represented by
(h,h^{\prime})N_{C_{i}}
where
h,h^{\prime}\in K\cap H
.

As a consequence, we see that any matrix-spherical function for
(G,H)
is
entirely determined by its restrictions to some standard Cartan subsets (Theorem
3.8
and Lemma
2.5
).
This defines for us an appropriate setting in which we
can now start computing
radial parts of the differential operators encountered in Lemma
2.2
, and especially of the quadratic Casimir element.

4.
Radial Parts

4.1.
Root Space Decomposition

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
C^{\prime}=\exp(\mathfrak{c}')t
be a standard Cartan subset of
G
. For

\alpha\in(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}})^{*}
define

\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}:=\{X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid\forall Z\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}:\quad\ad(Z)(X)=\alpha(Z)X\}.

Then

(i)

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{\alpha}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
;

(ii)

\commutator{\mathfrak{g}_\alpha}{\mathfrak{g}_\beta}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha+\beta}
;

(iii)

\sigma(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}

(iv)

B(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{g}_{\beta})=0
unless
\alpha+\beta=0
.

Write
\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
for the set of
\alpha
with

\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})>0
.

Proof.

(i)

Since
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
is commutative, so is
\ad(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
.
For simultaneous diagonalisability and hence the existence of the claimed
root space decomposition, it suffices therefore to show semisimplicity.
We show that there is a (sesquilinear, positive-definite) inner product on

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
with respect to which
\ad(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
acts normally.

Recall that since
(G,K,\theta,B)
is a reductive Lie group, the bilinear
form
B_{\theta}
is a positive-definite inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
. If
we extend it sesquilinearly, we therefore obtain an inner product on

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
. For any
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
Z\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}

we have

\displaystyle B_{\theta}(\ad(Z)(X),Y)

\displaystyle=-B(\ad(Z)(X),\theta(Y))=B(X,\ad(Z)(\theta(Y)))

\displaystyle=B(X,\theta(\ad(\theta(Z))(Y)))=-B_{\theta}(X,\ad(\theta(Z))(Y)).

Consequently, the adjoint of
\ad(Z)
(with respect to
B_{\theta}
)
is
-\ad(\theta(Z))
. Since by definition,
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
is

\theta
-invariant, the element
-\ad(\theta(Z))
lies in the commutative
algebra
\ad(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
and therefore commutes with
\ad(Z)
.

(ii)

Let
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},Y\in\mathfrak{g}_{\beta}
and

Z\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
, then

\displaystyle\ad(Z)(\commutator{X}{Y})

\displaystyle=\commutator{\ad(Z)(X)}{Y}+\commutator{X}{\ad(Z)(Y)}

\displaystyle=\commutator{\alpha(Z)X}{Y}+\commutator{X}{\beta(Z)Y}

\displaystyle=(\alpha+\beta)(Z)\commutator{X}{Y}.

(iii)

Let
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},Z\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
. By definition
of
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
, we have
\sigma(Z)=-Z
, hence

\ad(Z)(\sigma(X))=\sigma(\ad(\sigma(Z))(X))=-\sigma(\ad(Z)(X))=-\alpha(Z)\sigma(X).

(iv)

Let
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},Y\in\mathfrak{g}_{\beta}
and
Z\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
, then

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=B(\ad(Z)(X),Y)+B(X,\ad(Z)(Y))

\displaystyle=\alpha(Z)B(X,Y)+\beta(Z)B(X,Y)

\displaystyle=(\alpha+\beta)(Z)B(X,Y)

due to
B
’s
\ad
-invariance. If
B(X,Y)\neq 0
, the above implies that

(\alpha+\beta)(Z)=0
for all
Z
, and hence
\alpha+\beta=0
.

∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\exp(\mathfrak{c}_1)t_{1},\exp(\mathfrak{c}_2)t_{2}
be two Cartan subsets
and suppose there exists
\phi\in\Aut(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
with

\phi(\mathfrak{c}_{1})=\mathfrak{c}_{2}
. Then
the reduced root systems
\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}_{1})=\phi^{*}(\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}_{2}))
and

\phi(\mathfrak{g}_{\phi^{*}(\alpha)})=\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

for all
\alpha\in(\mathfrak{c}_{2,\mathbb{C}})^{*}
, meaning that the root
systems and root multiplicities are the same.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in(\mathfrak{c}_{2,\mathbb{C}})^{*}
, then
\phi^{*}(\alpha)=\alpha\circ\phi\in(\mathfrak{c}_{1,\mathbb{C}})^{*}
.
Let
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\phi^{*}(\alpha)}
and
Z\in\mathfrak{c}_{1}
, then

\ad(Z)(\phi(X))=\phi(\ad(\phi^{-1}(Z))(X))=\phi(\phi^{*}(\alpha)(\phi^{-1}(Z))X)=\alpha(Z)\phi(X),

which shows that
\phi(X)\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. Consequently,

\phi
restricts to isomorphisms
\mathfrak{g}_{\phi^{*}(\alpha)}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in(\mathfrak{c}_{2,\mathbb{C}})^{*}
,
which in turn also shows that the root systems are isomorphic via
\phi^{*}
.
∎

4.2.
Decomposition

From now on we shall fix a standard Cartan subset
C^{\prime}=\exp(\mathfrak{c}')t
. Assume that
\Ad(t)
leaves

\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
invariant and can be decomposed
as follows: there is an involution
\phi\in O(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},B)
that
commutes with
\sigma
, and
\epsilon:\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that

\forall\alpha\in\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}),X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}:\quad\Ad(t)(X)=\epsilon_{\alpha}\phi(X).

By Lemma
3.5
,
\Ad(t)
is an involution of

\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
and hence also of
\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
. Unless this leads to ambiguity, we shall denote this
involution by
t
.

This definition implies two facts about the function
\epsilon
:

Lemma 4.3
.

For
\alpha\in\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
we have

(i)

\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{-\alpha}=1
and

(ii)

\epsilon_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{t\alpha}
.

In particular,
\epsilon_{0}=\pm 1
, which can be absorbed into

\phi
.

Proof.

(i)

Let
0\neq X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
, then due to the
non-degeneracy of
B
there exists
Y\in\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}

such that
B(X,Y)\neq 0
. Then due to
B
’s invariance under

\Ad(t)
and
\phi
we have

\displaystyle B(X,Y)

\displaystyle=B(\Ad(t)X,\Ad(t)Y)=B(\epsilon_{\alpha}\phi(X),\epsilon_{-\alpha}\phi(Y))

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{-\alpha}B(\phi(X),\phi(Y))=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{-\alpha}B(X,Y),

hence
1=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{-\alpha}
since

B(X,Y)\neq 0
.

(ii)

Let
0\neq X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
, then we leverage
that
\sigma(t)=t^{-1}
and that
\sigma(\Ad(t)(X))\in\mathfrak{g}_{-t\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\sigma(X)

\displaystyle=\Ad(t)\Ad(t)^{-1}\sigma(X)=\Ad(t)\sigma\Ad(t)(X)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{-t\alpha}\phi\sigma\Ad(t)(X)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{-t\alpha}\phi\sigma\phi(X)=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{-t\alpha}\sigma(X),

which shows with (i) that
\epsilon_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{t\alpha}
.

∎

We now investigate what
Z_{C^{\prime}}
and
N_{C^{\prime}}
look and act like.

Proposition 4.4
.

(i)

(h,h^{\prime})\in Z_{C^{\prime}}
if and only if
h^{\prime}=t^{-1}ht
and

\Ad(h)
acts trivially on
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

(h,h^{\prime})\in N_{C^{\prime}}
if and only if
\Ad(h)\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
and
hth^{\prime-1}t^{-1}\in\exp(\mathfrak{c}')
.

(iii)

If
(h,h^{\prime})\in N_{C^{\prime}}
, say
hth^{\prime-1}t^{-1}=\exp(Y)
,
then
(h,h^{\prime})
acts on
C^{\prime}
as follows:

(h,h^{\prime})\cdot\exp(X)t=\exp(\Ad(h)(X) + Y)t

Proof.

(i)

“
\Rightarrow
”: We know
t\in C^{\prime}
, then
hth^{\prime-1}=t
,
whence
h^{\prime}=t^{-1}ht
. Let
X\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\derivative{s}\evaluated{\exp(tX)tt^{-1}}_{s=0}=\derivative{s}\evaluated{h\exp(sX)th^{\prime-1}t^{-1}}_{s=0}

\displaystyle=\derivative{s}\evaluated{\exp(s\Ad(h)(X))}_{s=0}=\Ad(h)(X).

“
\Leftarrow
”: Let
(h,h^{\prime})\in H\times H
satisfy the conditions above.
Every element of
C^{\prime}
can be written as
\exp(X)t
, so that

(h,h^{\prime})\cdot\exp(X)t=h\exp(X)th^{\prime-1}=\exp(\Ad(h)(X))hth^{\prime-1}=\exp(X)t,

so that
(h,h^{\prime})\in Z_{C^{\prime}}
.

(ii)

“
\Rightarrow
”: We apply the definition first to
t
, which
yields

hth^{\prime-1}=\exp(Y)t

for some
Y\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
. Let now
X\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
and

s\in\mathbb{R}
, then

h\exp(sX)th^{\prime-1}=\exp(\Ad(h)(sX) + Y)t.

Multiplying with
t^{-1}
and taking the
s
-derivative at
s=0
, shows
that
\Ad(h)(X)\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
. The reverse implication is clear.

(iii)

Follows from (ii).

∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}:=Z_{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
. Then

\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}.

This direct sum is orthogonal with respect to
B
and
B_{\sigma}
.

Proof.

The sum is evidently direct as
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
and

\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
lie in different eigenspaces of
\sigma
. The
inclusion “
\supseteq
” is clear. For “
\subseteq
”,
note that
\sigma
leaves
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
invariant by
Proposition
4.1
(iii), hence we can decompose

\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{-},

where
\sigma
acts as
\pm 1
on
\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{\pm}
. Since

\sigma
is orthogonal with respect to both
B
and
B_{\sigma}
,
these two eigenspaces are orthogonal with respect to both bilinear
forms. It just remains to show that

\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{+}\subset\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{-}\subset\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
(then these inclusions are equalities
and we get the claimed orthogonality).

For the first inclusion, let
X+iY\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{+}
, then both

X
and
Y
commute with
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
and satisfy

\sigma(X)=X,\sigma(Y)=Y
, so that
X,Y\in\mathfrak{h}
, and hence

X,Y\in\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
.

For the second inclusion, let
X+iY\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{-}
, then

X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\sigma(\Ad(t^{-1})(X))

\displaystyle=\sigma(\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\phi(X))=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\phi\sigma(X)=-\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\phi(X)

\displaystyle=-\Ad(t^{-1})(X),

so
X\in\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
as well. Consequently,

X\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(t)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})

and commutes with
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
is
maximal commutative, we have
X\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
. Similarly, we also
see
Y\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
.
∎

Definition 4.6
.

Let
x\in\exp(X)t\in C^{\prime}
and
\alpha\in\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
. Define

x^{\alpha}:=\epsilon_{\alpha}\exp(\alpha(X))\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

Proposition 4.7
.

(i)

For
x\in C^{\prime}
and
Y\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
we have

\Ad(x)(Y)=x^{t\alpha}\phi(Y)\qquad\Ad(x^{-1})(Y)=x^{-\alpha}\phi(Y),

so in particular
x\mapsto x^{\alpha}

is well-defined.

(ii)

For all
x\in C^{\prime},\alpha\in\Sigma(\mathfrak{g}:\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
we have
(x^{\alpha})^{-1}=x^{-\alpha}
.

(iii)

With respect to the group homomorphism

\exp(\mathfrak{c}')\to\mathbb{C}^{\times},\qquad\exp(X')\mapsto\exp(\alpha(X')),

the map
x\mapsto x^{\alpha}
intertwines the (left) group actions
of
\exp(\mathfrak{c}')
on
C^{\prime}
and of

\mathbb{C}^{\times}
on
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
(is in particular a homomorphism
of torsors).

(iv)

Let
(h,h^{\prime})\in N_{C^{\prime}}
and let

hth^{\prime-1}=x=\exp(Y)t
. Then

\quantity(\epsilon_\alpha x^{-\Ad^*(h)(\alpha)})^{2}=1.

Proof.

(i)

We have

\Ad(x)(Y)=\exp(\ad(X))\Ad(t)(Y)=\epsilon_{\alpha}\exp(\ad(X))\phi(Y)=\epsilon_{\alpha}\exp((t\alpha)(X))\phi(Y),

which by Lemma
4.3
(ii) equals

x^{t\alpha}\phi(Y)
as claimed. Furthermore, we have

\Ad(x^{-1})(Y)=\Ad(t^{-1})\exp(\ad(-X))(Y)=\epsilon_{t\alpha}^{-1}\exp(-\alpha(X))\phi(Y)=x^{-\alpha}\phi(Y)

by Lemma
4.3
(i,ii).

(ii)

Follows from Lemma
4.3
(i).

(iii)

Follows from the definition and the fact that

\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
is commutative.

(iv)

Rearranging the definition of
x
we obtain

h=xh^{\prime}t^{-1}
. Applying
\sigma
, this equals

tx^{-1}t^{-1}h^{\prime}t
. Writing
x=\exp(Y)t
, this equation becomes

\exp(Y)th^{\prime}t^{-1}=\exp(-Y)t^{-1}h^{\prime}t.

Let
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
, then

\Ad(th^{\prime}t^{-1})(X)
and
\Ad(t^{-1}h^{\prime}t)(X)
only differ by
a constant:

\displaystyle\Ad(th^{\prime}t^{-1})(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon_{\Ad(th^{\prime}t)(\alpha)}}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}\phi(\Ad(h^{\prime})(\phi(X)))

\displaystyle\Ad(t^{-1}h^{\prime}t)(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon_{\alpha}}{\Ad(tht^{\prime})(\alpha)}\phi(\Ad(h^{\prime})(\phi(X))).

Consequently, we have

\exp(\Ad^*(th't)(\alpha)(Y))\frac{\epsilon_{\Ad(th^{\prime}t)}}{\epsilon_{\alpha}}=\exp(-\Ad^*(th't)(\alpha)(Y))\frac{\epsilon_{\alpha}}{\epsilon_{\Ad(th^{\prime}t)(\alpha)}},

which implies the claim using the fact that

\Ad^{*}(th^{\prime}t)
and
\Ad^{*}(h)
coincide on
(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}})^{*}
.

∎

Lemma 4.8
.

We can characterise
C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
as follows:

C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}=\{x\in C^{\prime}\mid\forall\alpha\in\Sigma:\quad x^{\alpha}\neq x^{-\alpha}\}.

Proof.

Let
x\in C^{\prime}
. By Lemma
3.7
we have

x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
iff
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
. So we are going to show
that this condition holds iff
x^{\alpha}\neq x^{-\alpha}
for all roots

\alpha\in\Sigma
.

“
\Rightarrow
”: Assume that

\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})=\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha\in\Sigma
and
0\neq E\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. Then

E-\sigma(E)\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}
is a nontrivial linear
combination of
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
-root vectors for different root spaces
and cannot be a root vector itself, in particular not an element
of
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
. This implies that
E-\sigma(E)\not\in\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
. We have

\Ad(x^{-1})(E-\sigma(E))=x^{-\alpha}\phi(E)-x^{\alpha}\phi(\sigma(E))=x^{-\alpha}\phi(E)-x^{\alpha}\sigma(\phi(E)).

That this element is not
\sigma
-antiinvariant, shows that

x^{\alpha}\neq x^{-\alpha}
.

“
\Leftarrow
”: Assume
x^{\alpha}\neq x^{-\alpha}
for all
roots
\alpha\in\Sigma
and let
Y\in\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma}\cap\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{g}^{-\sigma})
. We can decompose
Y
according
to

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

as

Y=Y_{0,+}+Y_{0,-}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}Y_{\alpha}.

That
Y
is
\sigma
-antiinvariant shows that

Y_{0,+}=0
and
Y_{-\alpha}=-\sigma(Y_{\alpha})
for all
\alpha\in\Sigma
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\Ad(x^{-1})(Y)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{0}\phi(Y_{0,-})+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\quantity(x^{-\alpha} \phi(Y_\alpha)
- x^\alpha\phi(\sigma(Y_\alpha)))

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{0}\phi(Y_{0,-})+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\quantity(x^{-\alpha} \phi(Y_\alpha)
- x^\alpha\sigma(\phi(Y_\alpha))).

Since this is
\sigma
-antiinvariant as well, we obtain

x^{-\alpha}\phi(Y_{\alpha})=x^{\alpha}\phi(Y_{\alpha})
. Since

x^{\alpha}\neq x^{-\alpha}
, this reads
\phi(Y_{\alpha})=0
or equivalently
Y_{\alpha}=0
. Consequently, we have

Y=Y_{0,-}\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
and
0\neq E\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. Let

H:=E+\sigma(E)
, then

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\frac{\Ad(x)(\phi(H))-x^{-\alpha}H}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}

\displaystyle\sigma(E)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{\alpha}H-\Ad(x)(\phi(H))}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}

\displaystyle\phi(E)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{\alpha}\phi(H)-\Ad(x^{-1})(H)}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}

\displaystyle\sigma(\phi(E))

\displaystyle=\frac{\Ad(x^{-1})(H)-x^{-\alpha}\phi(H)}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}

Proof.

From Proposition
4.7
we get

\displaystyle\Ad(x)(\phi(H))

\displaystyle=x^{\alpha}E+x^{-\alpha}\sigma(E)

\displaystyle\Ad(x^{-1})(H)

\displaystyle=x^{-\alpha}\phi(E)+x^{\alpha}\sigma(\phi(E)),

which leads to the following linear systems of equations.

\displaystyle\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix H\\\Ad(x)(\phi(H))\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & 1\\x^\alpha& x^{-\alpha}\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix E\\\sigma(E)\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\phi(H)\\\Ad(x^{-1})(H)\endlx@physics@matrix)

\displaystyle=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & 1\\x^{-\alpha} & x^\alpha\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\phi(E)\\\sigma(\phi(E))\endlx@physics@matrix).

Solving these systems yields the claimed expressions. Note that
by Lemma
4.8
, we always have

x^{\alpha}\neq x^{-\alpha}
, whence the determinant is nonzero.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

For each
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
we can decompose
\mathfrak{g}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}+\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}))

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}+\Ad(x^{-1})(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}})),

where the intersection of the last two summands is
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

We are showing the claims for the first decomposition. The others
follow by applying
\Ad(x^{-1})
. Note that we assumed that

\Ad(t)
leave
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
(and its complexification) invariant. Since
\Ad(t)
is orthogonal
with respect to
B
, and because of Lemma
4.5
, it therefore also leaves
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}

(and its complexification) invariant.

We evidently have the inclusion “
\supseteq
”, and the inclusion
“
\subseteq
” follows from Proposition
4.9
. So it remains to show that

\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\cap(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}+\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}))=0

and that

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}})=\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}.

For the first note that
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
and

\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}
are orthogonal with respect to
B
because they
lie in different eigenspaces of
\sigma
, which is orthogonal with
respect to
B
. Since
B
restricted to
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
is also
non-degenerate, we conclude that
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}=0
. Applying
\Ad(x)
and the fact that

\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
is invariant under
\Ad(x)
yields the desired
directness of the sum.

Let
Y\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}})
. We expand

Y=Y_{0,+}+Y_{0,-}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}Y_{\alpha}.

Then due to
\sigma
-invariance we have
Y_{0,-}=0
and

Y_{-\alpha}=\sigma(Y_{\alpha})
. Applying
\Ad(x^{-1})
yields

\Ad(x^{-1})(Y)=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\phi(Y_{0,+})+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{+}}\quantity(x^{-\alpha} \phi(Y_\alpha)
+ x^\alpha\sigma(\phi(Y_\alpha))).

The fact that this is also
\sigma
-invariant and

x^{\alpha}\neq x^{-\alpha}
(by Lemma
4.8
) yields that
Y_{\alpha}=0
, hence also
Y_{-\alpha}
, so that

Y=Y_{0,+}\in\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

For
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
we can decompose

U(\mathfrak{g})=\Ad(x^{-1})(U(\mathfrak{h}))S(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})U(\mathfrak{h})=U(\mathfrak{h})S(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\Ad(x)(U(\mathfrak{h})).

with the only ambiguity being
U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
acting
on
\Ad(x^{-1})(U(\mathfrak{h}))
(resp.

U(\mathfrak{h}))
) from the left and on

U(\mathfrak{h})
(resp.
\Ad(x)(U(\mathfrak{h}))
) from the right.

Proof.

From Lemma
4.10
we obtain that if

\mathfrak{q}
is a
B
-orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}

in
\mathfrak{h}
, we have

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\Ad(x)(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{C}})
(and the same expression with
x
inverted). By the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt theorem the multiplication map generates isomorphisms

U(\mathfrak{g})\cong\Ad(x^{-1})(U(\mathfrak{q}))\otimes U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})\otimes S(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\otimes U(\mathfrak{q})\cong U(\mathfrak{q})\otimes U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})\otimes U(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\otimes\Ad(x)(U(\mathfrak{q})).

Applying the same to
U(\mathfrak{h})
we see that

U(\mathfrak{h})=U(\mathfrak{q})\otimes U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})=U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})\otimes U(\mathfrak{q})
and

\Ad(x^{-1})(U(\mathfrak{h}))=\Ad(x^{-1})(U(\mathfrak{q}))\otimes U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}),\qquad\Ad(x)(U(\mathfrak{h}))=U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})\otimes\Ad(x)(U(\mathfrak{q})),

so that the above decompositions become

\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})

\displaystyle=\Ad(x^{-1})(U(\mathfrak{h}))\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})}\quantity(S(\mathfrak{c}')\otimes U(\mathfrak{h}))

\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{g})

\displaystyle=\quantity(U(\mathfrak{h})\otimes S(\mathfrak{c}'))\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})}\Ad(x)(U(\mathfrak{h})),

where
U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
acts only on the
U(\mathfrak{h})
components.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

There are maps

\Pi,\widetilde{\Pi}:\,U(\mathfrak{g})\to C^{\infty}(C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs})\otimes S(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\otimes(U(\mathfrak{h})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})}U(\mathfrak{h}))

such
that for

\Pi(p)=\sum_{i}f_{i}\otimes p_{i}\otimes u_{i}\otimes v_{i}\qquad\widetilde{\Pi}(u)=\sum_{j}\tilde{f}_{j}\otimes\tilde{p}_{j}\otimes\tilde{u}_{j}\otimes\tilde{v}_{j}

we have

p=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\Ad(x^{-1})(u_{i}p_{i})v_{i}=\sum_{j}\tilde{f}_{j}(x)\tilde{u}_{j}\tilde{p}_{j}\Ad(x)(\tilde{v}_{j})

for all
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
. Here,
U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
acts on
the two
U(\mathfrak{h})
-tensor factors as follows: the right action (on the 1st factor) is twisted by
\Ad(t)
, and the left
action is just the usual multiplication.

Proof.

Follows from Corollary
4.11
and
Proposition
4.9
. For the
U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
-actions note that we can write

p=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\Ad(x^{-1})(u_{i}m_{i}p_{i})v_{i}

where
u_{i},v_{i}
are written entirely in terms of
\mathfrak{q}
,
and
m_{i}\in U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
, then this equals

=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\Ad(x^{-1})(u_{i}p_{i})\Ad(t^{-1})(m_{i})v_{i},

meaning that
\Pi
maps
p
to both

\sum_{i}f_{i}\otimes p_{i}\otimes u_{i}m_{i}\otimes v_{i}

and

\sum_{i}f_{i}\otimes p_{i}\otimes u_{i}\otimes\Ad(t^{-1})v_{i},

which should be equal. Similarly for
\widetilde{\Pi}
.
∎

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
W
be a finite-dimensional
H
-bimodule and write

\pi_{\mathrm{L}},\pi_{\mathrm{R}}
for the left and right actions, respectively (
\pi_{\mathrm{R}}
is then a representation of
H^{\operatorname{op}}
).
There are maps

R^{W},\widetilde{R}^{W}:\>U(\mathfrak{g})\to C^{\infty}(C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs})\otimes S(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\otimes\Hom(W^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}},W)

such that for every
f\in E^{W}(G,H)
,
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
, and

p\in U(\mathfrak{g})
we have

\displaystyle(p\cdot f)(x)

\displaystyle=R^{W}(p)(x)(f|_{C^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle(f\cdot p)(x)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{R}^{W}(p)(x)(f|_{C^{\prime}})(x).

In particular, for
p\in U(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
, the
matrix parts of
R^{W},\widetilde{R}^{W}
lie in

\End(W^{\mathfrak{m^{\prime}}})
. Here,
Y\in\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
acts as follows on
v\in W
:

Y\cdot_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}v=\Ad(t)(Y)\cdot v-v\cdot Y.

Proof.

Define
R^{W},\widetilde{R}^{W}
by post-composing
\Pi,\widetilde{\Pi}
with the representations
\pi_{\mathrm{L}},\pi_{\mathrm{R}}
as follows: the last two tensor legs,
U(\mathfrak{h})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})}U(\mathfrak{h})
act as follows on

W^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
:

(a\otimes b)\cdot\psi=a\cdot\psi\cdot b.

This is well-defined precisely because
f
is an
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
-homomorphism twisted in the correct way.

We now show that
R^{W},\widetilde{R}^{W}
indeed yield the
radial parts of the left and right actions on matrix-spherical functions. Let
f\in E^{W}(G,H),x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs},p\in U(\mathfrak{g})
, say

\Pi(p)=\sum_{i}f_{i}\otimes p_{i}\otimes u_{i}\otimes v_{i},

so that

\forall x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}:\quad\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\Ad(x^{-1})(u_{i}p_{i})v_{i}.

For any
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
we then have

(p\cdot f)(x)=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\quantity(\Ad(x^{-1})(u_ip_i)v_i\cdot f)(x)=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\quantity(v_i\cdot f\cdot u_ip_i)(x)

by Lemma
2.2
. Corollary
2.4
then allows us to write this as

\displaystyle(p\cdot f)(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)u_{i}\cdot(f\cdot p_{i})(x)\cdot v_{i}

\displaystyle=R(p)(x)f(x),

where we interpret
S(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})
as differential operators acting
on
C^{\infty}(G,W)
via right-invariant vector fields.

To see that
R^{W}(p)
for
p\in U(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
preserves pointwise
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
-invariance, let
Y\in\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
, and note that

\displaystyle\Ad(t)(Y)\circ(p\cdot f)(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\Ad(t)(Y)u_{i}\cdot(p_{i}\cdot f)(x)\cdot v_{i}

\displaystyle=\quantity(\sum_i f_i(x) Y\Ad(x^{-1})(u_i)p_iv_i\cdot f)(x)

\displaystyle=\quantity(Y p\cdot f)(x).

Now, since
p
commutes with
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
, this equals

\displaystyle\quantity(pY\cdot f)(x)

\displaystyle=\quantity(\sum_i f_i(x) \Ad(x^{-1})(u_i) p_iv_iY\cdot f)(x)=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)u_{i}\cdot(f\cdot p_{i})(x)\cdot v_{i}Y

\displaystyle=(p\cdot f)(x)\cdot Y.

The statements about
\widetilde{R}^{W}
follow analogously.
∎

Recall from Lemma
2.7
that the restriction
of a MSF maps not only to
W^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
, but even to

W^{Z_{C^{\prime}}}
. If we consider that invariance, we get more out of the previous result.

Definition 4.14
.

Define

M^{\prime}:=Z_{H}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\cap t^{-1}Ht.

This is a closed subgroup of
H
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
,
since
\Ad(t^{-1})
leaves
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
invariant.

Proposition 4.15
.

The map
h\mapsto(tht^{-1},h)
is a (well-defined) isomorphism

M^{\prime}\cong Z_{C^{\prime}}
that intertwines between the partially twisted
action of
M^{\prime}
on
W
and the action of
Z_{C^{\prime}}
on
W
. In particular,

W^{Z_{C^{\prime}}}
is a subspace of
W^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Note that by definition of
M^{\prime}
, both
tht^{-1}
and
h
are
elements of
H
, so the map is well-defined. Furthermore, by
Proposition
4.4
, it is an isomorphism.

The partially twisted action of
M^{\prime}
on
W
is defined
so as to differentiate to the one of
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
:

h\cdot_{M^{\prime}}\psi=tht^{-1}\cdot\psi\cdot h^{-1},

which is exactly how
(tht^{-1},h)\in Z_{C^{\prime}}
acts on

\psi\in W
.

This shows that
W^{Z_{C^{\prime}}}=W^{M^{\prime}}
is a subspace
of
W^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
, which is equal if
M^{\prime}
is the
analytic subgroup generated by
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
(i.e. is connected).
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

R^{W}
and
\widetilde{R}^{W}
restrict to maps

U(\mathfrak{g})^{M^{\prime}}\to C^{\infty}(C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs})\otimes S(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\otimes\End(W^{M^{\prime}}).

Proof.

Let
f\in E^{W}(G,H)
and
p\in U(\mathfrak{g})^{M^{\prime}}
.
Let
m\in M^{\prime}
and
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
, then

\displaystyle tmt^{-1}\cdot(p\cdot f)(x)\cdot m^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)tmt^{-1}\cdot u_{i}\cdot(f\cdot p_{i})(x)\cdot v_{i}\cdot m^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)(v_{i}\cdot m^{-1}\cdot f\cdot tmt^{-1}\cdot u_{i}p_{i})(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\quantity(m^{-1}\cdot\Ad(m)(v_i)
\cdot f\cdot\Ad(tmt^{-1})(u_ip_i)\cdot tmt^{-1})(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\quantity(\Ad(m)(v_i)
\cdot f\cdot\Ad(tmt^{-1})(u_ip_i))(tmt^{-1}xm^{-1}).

By Lemma
2.2
, this equals

=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\quantity(\Ad(mx^{-1})(u_ip_i)\Ad(m)(v_i)\cdot f)(tmt^{-1}xm^{-1}).

By Propositions
4.15
and
4.4
, this equals

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)\quantity(\Ad(m)(\Ad(x^{-1})(u_ip_i)v_i)\cdot f)(x)

\displaystyle=(\Ad(m)(p)\cdot f)(x)=(p\cdot f)(x)

since
p
is invariant under
M^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
V,W
be finite-dimensional
H
-modules and assume that

V,\overline{W}
(
\overline{W}
is the same as
W
, but with the
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
-action twisted by
\Ad(t)
) are semisimple
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
-modules. Then

(\Hom(V,W))^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}=\Hom_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(V,\overline{W})=\bigoplus_{\rho\in\widehat{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}}\mathbb{C}^{[V:\rho][\overline{W}:\rho]}=:\mathcal{V}.

In that case, the maps
R^{W},\widetilde{R}^{W}
map to

U(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\to C^{\infty}(C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs})\otimes S(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\otimes\End(\mathcal{V}).

An analogous statement holds when we define
\mathcal{V}
in
terms of the group
M^{\prime}
instead of
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
.

4.3.
Radial Part of the Quadratic Casimir Element
\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}

In this subsection we will now compute
\Pi
of the quadratic Casimir
element (
\widetilde{\Pi}
will turn out to be the same). The application to MSF for a concrete
H
-bimodule
W
is then straightforward.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
\alpha\in(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}})^{*}
. Write
C_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}

for the unique element
X
such that

\forall Y\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}:\quad\alpha(Y)=B(X,Y).

This exists since due to Proposition
4.1
(iv) and
Lemma
4.5

B
is non-degenerate when restricted to

\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
and
H:=E+\sigma(E)
.

(i)

Then
\commutator{E}{\sigma(E)}=-B_{\sigma}(E,E)C_{\alpha}
.

(ii)

For
x\in C^{\prime}
we have
\commutator{H}{\Ad(x)(\phi(H))}=\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})B_{\sigma}(E,E)C_{\alpha}
.

(iii)

For
x\in C^{\prime}
we have
\commutator{\Ad(x^{-1})(H)}{\phi(H)}=\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})B_{\sigma}(E,E)C_{t\alpha}
.

Proof.

(i)

From Proposition
4.1
(iii,ii) we
see that
\commutator{E}{\sigma(E)}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. Furthermore,
since
\sigma
is an involutive Lie algebra homomorphism, we
have
\sigma(\commutator{E}{\sigma(E)})=-\commutator{E}{\sigma(E)}
, so that
by Lemma
4.5
, we have

\commutator{E}{\sigma(E)}\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
Y\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
, then

B(Y,\commutator{E}{\sigma(E)})=B(\commutator{Y}{E},\sigma(E))=\alpha(Y)B(E,\sigma(E))=-\alpha(Y)B_{\sigma}(E,E),

hence
\commutator{E}{\sigma(E)}=-B_{\sigma}(E,E)C_{\alpha}
.

(ii)

From the proof of Proposition
4.9
we know that

\commutator{H}{\Ad(x)(\phi(H))}=\commutator{E + \sigma(E)}{x^\alpha E + x^{-\alpha}\sigma(E)}=-\quantity(x^\alpha- x^{-\alpha})\commutator{E}{\sigma(E)},

which together with (i) implies the claim.

(iii)

From the proof of Proposition
4.9
we
know that

\displaystyle\commutator{\Ad(x^{-1})(H)}{\phi(H)}

\displaystyle=\commutator{x^{-\alpha}\phi(E) + x^\alpha\sigma(\phi(E))}{\phi(E) + \sigma(\phi(E))}

\displaystyle=-\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})\commutator{\phi(E)}{\sigma(\phi(E))}.

Together with (i) applied to
\phi(E)
, the fact that
B_{\sigma}(\phi(E),\phi(E))=B_{\sigma}(E,E)
, and the fact that

\phi(E)\in\mathfrak{g}_{t\alpha}
, the claim follows.

∎

Corollary 4.20
.

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
and
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
. Write

H:=E+\sigma(E)
. Then

\displaystyle\anticommutator{E}{\sigma(E)}

\displaystyle=-2\frac{H^{2}+\Ad(x)(\phi(H)^{2})-\quantity(x^\alpha+x^{-\alpha})H\Ad(x)(\phi(H))}{\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad-B_{\sigma}(E,E)\frac{x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}C_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\anticommutator{\phi(E)}{\sigma(\phi(E))}

\displaystyle=-2\frac{\phi(H)^{2}+\Ad(x^{-1})(H^{2})-\quantity(x^\alpha+x^{-\alpha})\Ad(x^{-1})(H)\phi(H)}{\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad-B_{\sigma}(E,E)\frac{x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}C_{t\alpha}

Proof.

By Proposition
4.9
we have

\displaystyle\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})^{2}\anticommutator{E}{\sigma(E)}

\displaystyle=\anticommutator{\Ad(x)(\phi(H)) - x^{-\alpha}H}{x^\alpha H - \Ad(x)(\phi(H))}

\displaystyle=-2H^{2}-2\Ad(x)(\phi(H)^{2})+\quantity(x^\alpha+x^{-\alpha})\anticommutator{H}{\Ad(x)(\phi(H))}.

We can write the anticommutator as

2H\Ad(x)(\phi(H))-\commutator{H}{\Ad(x)(\phi(H))}
, so that by
Lemma
4.19
we have

=-2H^{2}-2\Ad(x)(\phi(H)^{2})+2\quantity(x^\alpha+x^{-\alpha})H\Ad(x)(\phi(H))-B_{\sigma}(E,E)\quantity(\qty(x^\alpha)^2 - \qty(x^{-\alpha})^2)C_{\alpha}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})\anticommutator{\phi(E)}{\sigma(\phi(E))}

\displaystyle=\anticommutator{x^\alpha\phi(H) - \Ad(x^{-1})(H)}{\Ad(x^{-1})(H)-x^{-\alpha}\phi(H)}

\displaystyle=-2\phi(H)^{2}-2\Ad(x^{-1})(H^{2})+\quantity(x^\alpha+ x^{-\alpha})\anticommutator{\Ad(x^{-1})(H)}{H}.

The anticommutator equals
2\Ad(x^{-1})(H)H-\commutator{\Ad(x^{-1})(H)}{H}
, so that by Lemma
4.19
, we have

\displaystyle=-2\phi(H)^{2}-2\Ad(x^{-1})(H^{2})+2\quantity(x^\alpha+x^{-\alpha})\Ad(x^{-1})(H)H

\displaystyle\qquad-B_{\sigma}(E,E)\quantity(\qty(x^\alpha)^2-\qty(x^{-\alpha})^2)C_{t\alpha}.\qed

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
\alpha\in\Sigma
, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
be an orthonormal basis of

\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
with respect to
B_{\sigma}
. Define

A_{\alpha}:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(X_{i}+\sigma(X_{i}))\otimes(X_{i}+\sigma(X_{i}))\in U(\mathfrak{h})\otimes U(\mathfrak{h}).

Then
A_{\alpha}
does not depend on the choice of basis. For

(h,h^{\prime})\in N_{C^{\prime}}
we have

\displaystyle(\Ad(h)\otimes\Ad(h))(A_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=A_{\Ad^{*}(h)(\alpha)}

\displaystyle(\Ad(h^{\prime})\otimes\Ad(h^{\prime}))(A_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=A_{\Ad^{*}(h^{\prime})(\alpha)}

and

(\Ad(h)\otimes\Ad(h^{\prime})\phi)(A_{\alpha})=\pm(1\otimes\phi)A_{\Ad^{*}(h)(\alpha)}

depending on whether

\epsilon_{\alpha}\quantity(hth^{\prime-1})^{-\Ad^{*}(h)(\alpha)}=\pm 1.

Proof.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{r}
be orthonormal bases, say

X_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{ij}Y_{j},

then
a_{ij}=B_{\sigma}(X_{i},Y_{j})
and in particular also

Y_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{ji}X_{j}
, so that

\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{ij}a_{ik}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}B_{\sigma}(a_{ik}X_{i},Y_{j})=B_{\sigma}(Y_{k},Y_{j})=\delta_{jk}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\quantity(X_i + \sigma(X_i))\otimes\quantity(X_i + \sigma(X_i))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}a_{ij}a_{ik}\quantity(Y_j + \sigma(Y_j))\otimes(Y_{k}+\sigma(Y_{k}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{ij}a_{ik}\quantity(Y_j + \sigma(Y_j))\otimes\quantity(Y_k+ \sigma(Y_k))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\quantity(Y_i + \sigma(Y_i))\otimes\quantity(Y_i +\sigma(Y_i)).

For any Lie algebra automorphism
\psi
that leaves

\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
and
B
invariant and commutes with
\sigma
(e.g.
\Ad(h)
or
\Ad(h^{\prime})
for

(h,h^{\prime})\in N_{C^{\prime}}
), the family
\phi(X_{1}),\dots,\phi(X_{r})
is a

B_{\sigma}
-orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}_{\phi^{*}\alpha}
, so

(\phi\otimes\phi)(A_{\alpha})=A_{\phi^{*}\alpha}.

Lastly, for
(\Ad(h)\otimes\Ad(h^{\prime})\phi)(A_{\alpha})
note that by
Proposition
4.7
(iv) we have

\epsilon_{\alpha}(hth^{\prime-1})^{-\Ad^{*}(h)(\alpha)}=\pm 1,

and in particular

\displaystyle\Ad(h)(X_{i})

\displaystyle=\pm\phi(\Ad(h^{\prime})(\phi(X_{i})))

\displaystyle\Ad(h)(\sigma(X_{i}))

\displaystyle=\pm\sigma(\phi(\Ad(h^{\prime})(\phi(X_{i})))),

so that we have

\displaystyle\quantity(\Ad(h)\otimes\Ad(h')\phi)A_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\pm\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\phi\Ad(h^{\prime})\phi\otimes\Ad(h^{\prime})\phi)(X_{i}+\sigma(X_{i}))\otimes(X_{i}+\sigma(X_{i}))

\displaystyle=\pm(1\otimes\phi)A_{\Ad^{*}(h)(\alpha)}

seeing as
\phi\Ad(h^{\prime})\phi
is orthogonal with respect to

B_{\sigma}
and commutes with
\sigma
, so that the expression in the
sum becomes the expression we used to define
A_{\alpha}
, except that

\phi\Ad(h^{\prime})\phi(X_{1}),\dots,\phi\Ad(h^{\prime})\phi(X_{r})

is now a
B_{\sigma}
-orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}_{\Ad^{*}(h)(\alpha)}
.
∎

Theorem 4.22
.

The decompositions of the quadratic Casimir element
\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}
with respect to the standard Cartan subset
C^{\prime}
are as follows:

\displaystyle\Pi(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})=\widetilde{\Pi}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{n_{\alpha}}{2}\coth_{\alpha}C_{\alpha}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch_{\alpha}^{2}}{4}\quantity(m(A_\alpha)\otimes 1 +
1\otimes m(A_{t\alpha}) + 2 (1\otimes\phi)A_\alpha)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch_{\alpha/2}^{2}}{4}(1\otimes\phi)A_{\alpha}.

where
m:U(\mathfrak{h})\otimes U(\mathfrak{h})\to U(\mathfrak{h})

is the multiplication map and
n_{\alpha}:=\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})
.

Proof.

Let
C_{1},\dots,C_{r}\in\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
and

M_{1},\dots,M_{s}\in\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
be orthonormal bases (with respect
to
B
). For
\alpha\in\Sigma
let

E_{\alpha,1},\dots,E_{\alpha,n_{\alpha}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
be
an orthonormal basis with respect to
B_{\sigma}
. Without loss of
generality assume that
E_{-\alpha,i}=\sigma(E_{\alpha},i)
. Then
the following bases are dual to each other with respect to
B
:

\displaystyle C_{1},\dots,C_{r},M_{1},\dots,M_{s},(E_{\alpha,1},\dots,E_{\alpha,n_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\in\Sigma}

\displaystyle C_{1},\dots,C_{r},M_{1},\dots,M_{s},(-\sigma(E_{\alpha,1}),\dots,-\sigma(E_{\alpha,n_{\alpha}}))_{\alpha\in\Sigma}.

Thus,

\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}C_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}M_{i}^{2}-\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\alpha}}E_{\alpha,i}\sigma(E_{\alpha_{i}}).

The first two sums are just
\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}
and
\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
, respectively. Symmetrising the summands over
\alpha
somewhat we obtain

\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\alpha}}\anticommutator{E_{\alpha,i}}{\sigma(E_{\alpha,i})}.

Let
x\in C^{\prime}\cap G_{rs}
. Substituting in the expression from Corollary
4.20
and setting
H_{\alpha,i}:=E_{\alpha,i}+\sigma(E_{\alpha,i})=E_{\alpha,i}+E_{-\alpha,i}
we get

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\alpha}}B_{\sigma}(E_{\alpha,i},E_{\alpha,i})\frac{x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}C_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\alpha}}\frac{H_{\alpha_{i}}^{2}+\Ad(x)(\phi(H_{\alpha,i})^{2})-(x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha})H_{\alpha,i}\Ad(x)(\phi(H_{\alpha,i}))}{\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{n_{\alpha}}{2}\frac{x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}C_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{m(A_{\alpha})+\Ad(x)(m(A_{t\alpha}))-(x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha})m(1\otimes\Ad(x)\phi)(A_{\alpha})}{\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})^{2}}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\alpha}}B_{\sigma}(\phi(E_{\alpha,i}),\phi(E_{\alpha,i}))\frac{x^{t\alpha}+x^{t\alpha}}{x^{t\alpha}-x^{-t\alpha}}\Ad(x^{-1})(C_{t\alpha})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\alpha}}\frac{H_{\alpha,i}^{2}+\Ad(x^{-1})(\phi(H_{\alpha,i})^{2})-(x^{t\alpha}+x^{-t\alpha})\Ad(x^{-1})(\phi(H_{\alpha,i}))H_{\alpha,i}}{\quantity(x^{t\alpha}-x^{-t\alpha})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{n_{\alpha}}{2}\frac{x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}\Ad(x^{-1})(C_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{m(A_{t\alpha})+\Ad(x^{-1})(m(A_{\alpha}))-(x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha})m\quantity(\Ad(x^{-1})\phi\otimes 1)A_{t\alpha}}{\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})^{2}}.

Note that these expressions are both in the shape that we can read
off
\Pi(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}),\widetilde{\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
Writing
\coth_{\alpha}(x):=\frac{x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha}}
and
\csch_{\alpha}(x):=2(x^{\alpha}-x^{-\alpha})^{-1}
,
we thus obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{n_{\alpha}}{2}\coth_{\alpha}C_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch^{2}_{\alpha}}{4}\quantity(m(A_\alpha)\otimes 1 +
1\otimes m(A_{t\alpha}))

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch_{\alpha}\coth_{\alpha}}{2}(1\otimes\phi)A_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\Pi(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{n_{\alpha}}{2}\coth_{\alpha}C_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch^{2}_{\alpha}}{4}\quantity(1\otimes m(A_{t\alpha}) + m(A_\alpha)\otimes 1)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch_{\alpha}\coth_{\alpha}}{2}(\phi\otimes 1)A_{t\alpha},

which equals
\widetilde{\Pi}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})
because

(\phi\otimes\phi)A_{\alpha}=A_{t\alpha}
(by Proposition
4.21
). Lastly, noting that

\displaystyle\frac{\csch_{\alpha}(x)\coth_{\alpha}(x)}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{\alpha}+x^{-\alpha}+2}{\quantity(x^\alpha-x^{-\alpha})}-\frac{\csch_{\alpha}^{2}(x)}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\quantity(1+x^\alpha)(1+x^{-\alpha})}{\quantity(1+x^\alpha)(1-x^{-\alpha})\quantity(1+x^{-\alpha})\quantity(x^{\alpha}-1)}-\frac{\csch_{\alpha}^{2}(x)}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\csch_{\alpha/2}^{2}(x)}{4}-\frac{\csch_{\alpha}^{2}(x)}{2},

where the square of
\csch_{\alpha/2}
is a well-defined quantity
obtained by multiplying out the product. In light of this we can
also rewrite
\Pi(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})=\widetilde{\Pi}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})
as

\displaystyle\Pi(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})=\widetilde{\Pi}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{n_{\alpha}}{2}\coth_{\alpha}C_{\alpha}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch_{\alpha}^{2}}{4}\quantity(m(A_\alpha)\otimes 1 +
1\otimes m(A_{t\alpha}) + 2 (1\otimes\phi)A_\alpha)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch_{\alpha/2}^{2}}{4}(1\otimes\phi)A_{\alpha}.

∎

Corollary 4.23
.

In the case where
\Ad(t)=1
, we have

(\exp(X)t)^{\alpha}=\exp(\alpha(X))
, and the radial part of

\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}
simplifies to

\displaystyle\Pi(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{n_{\alpha}}{2}\coth_{\alpha}C_{\alpha}+\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch_{\alpha}^{2}}{4}\quantity(m(A_\alpha)\otimes 1 +
1\otimes m(A_{\alpha}) + 2 A_\alpha)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\frac{\csch_{\alpha/2}^{2}}{4}A_{\alpha}

where
\coth_{\alpha},\csch_{\alpha}
are related to the usual
hyperbolic functions: if
x=\exp(X)
, then

\coth_{\alpha}(x)=\coth(\alpha(X)),\qquad\csch_{\alpha}(x)=\csch(\alpha(X)).

5.
Mathematical Setup for Conformal Blocks

Let
p+q=d>2
be natural numbers with
p\geq q
. Most commonly, we will encounter

q=0
(
Euclidean
) or
q=1
(
\emph{Lorentzian}
). Let furthermore

\eta
denote the standard bilinear form of signature
(p,q)
on

\mathbb{R}^{p,q}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and by abuse of notation also the one of signature
(p+1,q+1)
on

\mathbb{R}^{p+1,q+1}=\mathbb{R}^{d+2}
.

We will use lower case Greek letters to denote indices pertaining to
\mathbb{R}^{p+1,q+1}
and lower case Latin letters for
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
. For both we shall
use the usual index raising and lowering conventions of physics (with the pseudo-inner product
\eta
), such that
A_{\mu}=A^{\mu}
if
\mu\leq p
and

A_{\mu}=-A^{\mu}
if
\mu>p
(analogously for
A_{i}
).

5.1.
Conformal Compactification

Definition 5.1
.

Write
q:\,\mathbb{R}^{p+1,q+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{d+1}
for the
projectivisation map. The real variety

\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}:=\{q(v)\mid v\in\mathbb{R}^{p+1,q+1}\setminus\{0\},\eta(v,v)=0\}

is called the
conformal compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
.

Note that since
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
is Zariski closed, it is also closed in
the Euclidean topology. Since
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{d+1}
is compact, that entails
that
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
is also compact, as the name would imply.

We first establish that
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
is a smooth manifold in a
useful sense and that it is indeed a compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
is an embedded
submanifold of
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{d+1}
. Furthermore, the map

\iota:\,\mathbb{R}^{p,q}\to\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}},\qquad v\mapsto(1-\eta(v,v)\,:\,2v\,:\,1+\eta(v,v))

is a diffeomorphism with
\iota(\mathbb{R}^{p,q})
, which is dense in
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
.

Proof.

Note that
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
is regular since
\eta
is nondegenerate.
Next, we define
f:\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}\cap\{(v_{0}:\dots:v_{d+1})\mid v_{0}+v_{d+1}\neq 0\}\to\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
by

(v_{0}\,:\,v\,:\,v_{d+1})\mapsto\frac{v}{v_{0}+v_{d+1}}.

This map is evidently well-defined and smooth. Note also that

f(\iota(v))=v

for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
. If
(v_{0}:v:v_{d+1})\in\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
,
we have
\eta(v,v)=-(v_{0}+v_{d+1})(v_{0}-v_{d+1})
, so that

\displaystyle\iota(f(v_{0}:v:v_{d+1}))

\displaystyle=\iota\quantity(\frac{v}{v_0+v_{d+1}})

\displaystyle=\quantity(1 - \frac{\eta(v,v)}{(v_0+v_{d+1})^2} : \frac{2v}{v_0+v_{d+1}}
: 1 + \frac{v,v}{(v_0+v_{d+1})^2})

\displaystyle=\quantity(v_0+v_{d+1} + v_0 - v_{d+1}: 2v: v_0+v_{d+1}-v_0 + v_{d+1})

\displaystyle=(v_{0}:v:v_{d+1}).

Consequently,
\iota
is a diffeomorphism and
f
is its inverse. To
see that
\iota(\mathbb{R}^{p,q})
is dense, note that any element of the form

(v_{0}\,:\,v\,:\,-v_{0})

can be reached as follows: if
v=0
, then the point
(1:0:-1)=\infty

can be reached as

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\iota(tw)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}(1-t^{2}\eta(w,w):2w:1+t^{2}\eta(w,w))

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}(\eta(w,w)-t^{-2}:-2t^{-2}w:-\eta(w,w)-t^{-2})

\displaystyle=(1:0:-1)

for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
with
\eta(w,w)\neq 0
. If
v\neq 0
, there
is a vector
w
such that
\eta(v,w)\neq 0
. By rescaling, we can choose
that inner product to be
-v_{0}
. Note that
\eta(v,v)=0
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\iota(w+vt)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}(1-\eta(w,w)+2tv_{0}:2w+2vt:1+\eta(w,w)-2tv_{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}((1-\eta(w,w))t^{-1}+2v_{0}:2t^{-1}w+2v:(1+\eta(w,w))t^{-1}-2v_{0}))

\displaystyle=(v_{0}:v:-v_{0}).\qed

5.2.
Conformal Group
G
and its Structure

Lemma 5.3
.

G:=SO(p+1,q+1)_{0}
is the biggest classical connected Lie group acting on

\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
by projective transformations.

Proof.

The group of projective transformations of
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{d+1}

is
PGL(d+2)
, i.e.
GL(d+2)/\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. Evidently, those that
leave
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
invariant are

\displaystyle\{g\in GL(d+2)\mid\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{p+1,q+1}:\quad\eta(v,v)=0\Rightarrow\eta(gv,gv)=0\}/\mathbb{R}^{\times}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g\in GL(d+2)\mid\forall v\in\mathbb{C}^{p+1,q+1}:\quad\eta(v,v)=0\Rightarrow\eta(gv,gv)=0\}/\mathbb{R}^{\times}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g\in GL(d+2)\mid\mathbb{V}(\eta)\subseteq\mathbb{V}(\eta\circ g)\}/\mathbb{R}^{\times},

where we used that we can also complexify
\eta
and
\mathbb{R}^{p+1,q+1}
and the fact that the real restriction of

\eta
’s zero set is Zariski dense. By Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz,

\mathbb{V}(\eta)\subseteq\mathbb{V}(\eta\circ g)
implies
that the polynomial
\eta\circ g
is a multiple of
\eta
(as the ideals generated by both are radical). Since they are both homogeneous
of degree 2, this multiple must be a nonzero scalar, and since

\eta,g
have real coefficients, it must be a nonzero real, i.e.

\{g\in GL(d+2)\mid\exists C\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}:\eta\circ g=C\eta\}/\mathbb{R}^{\times}

is the group of projective transformations leaving
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{p,q}}
invariant. Since positive numbers have arbitrary
roots in
\mathbb{R}
, we can get rid of some of the actions

\{g\in GL(d+2)\mid\eta\circ g=\pm\eta\}/\{\pm 1\},

thus this group is doubly covered by an extension of
O(p+1,q+1)

by
\{\pm 1\}
. If we take the unit component, we get a group
that is (potentially) doubly covered by
SO(p+1,q+1)_{0}=G
.
∎

We therefore see that it is reasonable to study
G
.

Lemma 5.4

(
[
4
, §VII.2, example 2]
)
.

Together with
K=SO(p+1)\times SO(q+1)
and the maps

\displaystyle\theta:\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{g},\qquad

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto-X^{T}=\eta X\eta

\displaystyle B:\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle X\otimes Y

\displaystyle\mapsto{\rm tr}(XY),

G
is a reductive Lie group.

Definition 5.5
.

For
\mu,\nu,\sigma,\rho=0,\dots,d+1
define

\mathchoice{(E_{\mu\nu})^{{{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{(E_{\mu\nu})^{{{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{(E_{\mu\nu})^{{{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}{(E_{\mu\nu})^{{{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}}}}=\delta^{\sigma}_{\nu}\eta_{\nu\rho}

(so that
\mathchoice{(\mathchoice{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}})}{(\mathchoice{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}})}{(\mathchoice{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}})}{(\mathchoice{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{E^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}})}=\delta^{\sigma}_{\mu}\delta^{\nu}_{\rho}
)
and

F_{\mu\nu}:=E_{\mu\nu}-E_{\nu\mu}.

The set
(F_{\mu\nu})_{0\leq\mu<\nu\leq d+1}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
and
a short calculation will convince the reader that

\commutator{F_{\mu\nu}}{F_{\rho\sigma}}=\eta_{\nu\rho}F_{\mu\sigma}+\eta_{\mu\sigma}F_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\mu\rho}F_{\nu\sigma}-\eta_{\nu\sigma}F_{\mu\rho}.

Note in addition that
F_{\mu\nu}=-F_{\nu\mu}
and that

\theta(F_{\mu\nu})=F^{\mu\nu}
, which equals
F_{\mu\nu}
if

\mu,\nu\leq p
or
p<\mu,\nu
, and which equals
-F_{\mu\nu}
otherwise.

Proposition 5.6
.

\mathfrak{k}
is spanned by
F_{\mu\nu}
where
\mu,\nu
are either both

\leq p
or both
>p
and
\mathfrak{p}
is spanned by
F_{\mu\nu}
met

0\leq\mu\leq p<\nu\leq d+1
. For
a:=0,\dots,q
define

D_{a}:=F^{a,d+1-a}=-F_{a,d+1-a}
. Then

\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\operatorname{span}\{D_{0},\dots,D_{q}\}

is a maximal commutative subspace of
\mathfrak{p}
.

Proof.

By previous observations, the
F_{\mu\nu}
are contained in
